ParlaMint-SI_2020-09-29-SDZ8-Redna-20.ana.u2	Honourable President of the National Assembly, Members of Parliament and Members of Parliament! We have before us a second consideration of the draft law on amendments and additions to the Law on the State Prosecutor's Office following a reduced procedure. The proposed amendments align the legal order of the Republic of Slovenia with the Regulation of the European Union on the European Union Agency for Judicial Cooperation in Criminal Matters (Eurostat), address the irregularities identified by the Court of Auditors in the implementation and reporting of the readiness and on-call of the State Prosecutor and further strengthen the role of the Head of State Prosecutor in ensuring the effectiveness and uniformity of prosecution. In view of the debate at the Committee's meeting and the explanation of the amendment tabled, I would like to point out that the proposed article does not constitute a departure from the institution of the single law enforcement policy, which is within the competence of the Attorney-General, but rather rather; the proposed regulation means the implementation of what is already defined in the single law enforcement policy on expulsions, which expressly stipulates that continuous monitoring of the use of powers to dismiss criminal charges will be established with a view to simplifying the prosecution's practice and properly directing the police in the event of negative prosecution decisions. Although the Attorney-General is aware of the importance of rejecting criminal charges for legal certainty, unfortunately this monitoring has only been established in one district state prosecutor's office, only as regards particular importance. A legislator who cannot interfere with the independence of the Attorney General may, by means of organisational measures within the Public Prosecutor's Office, provide legal certainty by law, which is his responsibility and duty, in particular when he detects a direct violation of human rights. The professional controls of the Supreme Public Prosecutor from 2011 onwards contain the following findings of infringements of the principle of equality referred to in Article 15 of the Constitution of Slovenia in the case of dismissals of criminal charges: in certain cases foreign nationals have been treated more favourably than nationals of the Republic of Slovenia and thus are not equal; in certain cases, an indictment has been unjustly brought; in many cases, the State Prosecutor has not carried out an assessment of the evidence collected, but has rejected the case, referring to the police's allegations that there is no criminal offence; in many cases, the State Prosecutor has not served the injured person; in many cases, the State Prosecutor has cited as a reason for rejection the wrong reason that the injured person as a subsidiary prosecutor may have misjudged the case. So it is true that rejection does not mean the end of the proceedings and is not really a judicata and that the injured person can take over the persecution. Of course, he can only do so if he is informed that his criminal conviction has been dismissed and that he is not misdirected with the indication of the wrong reason for his rejection. Nor does the proposal constitute a violation of the principle of a stand-alone state prosecutor, otherwise this infringement would already be today, because, in accordance with the current regulation of the Head of State Prosecutors on the basis of a discretionary right, they decide which cases state prosecutors are obliged to submit for review, and these are very large. It is not a de facto decision to take a look at the leader not after the present or not after the proposed arrangement. Independence is guaranteed through a legal remedy that the Attorney General has before the State Prosecutor's Council. We also cannot agree that political pressure would be exerted through the heads of state's prosecutions on the basis of the proposed regulation, as, according to Justice Scoreboard's results over the past years, the state's prosecutor's office is doing well. At the same time, trust is very low due to political influences, which, if we accept both the conclusion and the truth, could mean that these pressures were carried out before the current government takes office and regardless of the current regime proposed. I therefore propose that, instead of telling the public that the heads of state's prosecutors are untrustworthy persons, we, too, should focus today's debate on strengthening the role of the state prosecutor's office and stressing their good functioning. Thank you.
ParlaMint-SI_2020-09-29-SDZ8-Redna-20.ana.u3	Thank you very much. The draft law was considered by the Committee on Justice as the working body of the mother. For the presentation of the report of the Committee, I give the floor to President Blaž Pavlin.
ParlaMint-SI_2020-09-29-SDZ8-Redna-20.ana.u4	President, thank you for your word. Good-bye, everybody! The Justice Committee is at its 18th session. 9. 2020 was considered by the National Labour Authority to be the draft law on amendments and additions to the Law on the State Prosecutor's Office, which was submitted by the Government to the National Assembly for consideration following a reduced procedure and was 3. Seven. 2020 published on the website of the National Assembly. The College of the President of the National Assembly shall be held at its 72nd session. Seven. 2020 decided that the proposal for a law should be examined in a simplified manner. The Committee was informed in advance of the opinion of the Legislative and Legal Service, the opinion of the State Prosecutor's Council, the opinion of the Commission of the State Council for State Procedure and the definition of the Ministry of Justice to the opinion of the Legislative and Legal Service. Representative of the proposer, Minister of Justice mag. Ms Kozlovič said in a complementary explanation of the articles that the proposed solutions aim to achieve the consistency, legality and enforceability of the EU regulation that changes the organisation of the European Union Agency for Judicial Cooperation in Criminal Matters (Eurostat) in the Slovenian legal order and the uniform application and interpretation of the legal provisions in practice. The mandate of the national representative and his deputy shall be extended and the appointment procedure shall be redefined. In addition, certain minor irregularities identified by the Court of Auditors in the implementation and recording of the state's readiness and on-calls are being addressed. In order to ensure effective implementation of the uniform law of prosecution and equal treatment of citizens, the draft law strengthens the responsibility of the Head of Public Prosecutions by supplementing the current regulation in Article 169 of the Public Prosecutor's Act with the obligation to submit a draft act in co-signed to the Head of Public Prosecutor's Office always prior to the decision of the State Prosecutor to dismiss an indictment for offences for which a prison sentence has been prescribed in the law for more than three years. The proposer supports the amendment, which, in view of the written comments made by the Legislative and Legal Services, has been tabled by the coalition parliamentary groups, which, instead of the signature, provides for an examination by the Head of State Prosecutor in cases of dismissal of complaints on the grounds that the alleged offence is not an offence that is being prosecuted under official law or that the suspect has not been duly suspected of having committed the alleged offence. The representative of the Legal and Legislative Service said that they had pointed out in a written legal opinion that there was a lack of definition of the schedule of assignments and the use of annual leave. However, the new institution co-signs the Head of Public Prosecutions to replace the current prior notification of the decision of the State Prosecutor could constitute a discrepancy with Article 135 of the Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia because it constitutes a form of active decision-making by the Head of State Prosecutor and thus a possible impact on the decisions of the individual State Prosecutor in a particular case. The functional independence of the State Prosecutor at systemic level is governed by the State Prosecutor's Act, which refers the status and status of the State Prosecutor to the position of a judge; it also follows from the explanation of the Constitutional Court's decision 2013 that the State Prosecutor is competent to decide, within the framework of the constitutional and legal framework, whether or not he will file a criminal charge and how he will represent it. The proposed amendment of the coalition parliamentary groups to Article 24 of the bill proposes a new second paragraph of Article 169 of the Law on the State Prosecutor's Office, which, according to the assessment of the Legislative and Legal Service, constitutes a departure from the law enforcement policy institute under the authority of the Attorney General. In the debate, opposition Members objected to the proposed regulation referred to in Article 24 of the novel and propose to delete it as it affects the independence of state prosecutors. As a result, it is questionable from a constitutional point of view and contrary to established international standards on the situation and functioning of state prosecutors in the exercise of the law enforcement function. The coalition Members supported the proposed legislative amendments, pointed out a large number of dropped complaints, following comments by the Legal and Legal Service and proposing an amendment to Article 24 that the State Prosecutor for Offences for which a prison sentence has been prescribed in the law for more than three years before the decision to dismiss the draft act must be submitted to the Head of State Prosecutor's Office for review. The Committee adopted the amendment of SDS, SMC, NSI and Desus to the 24th Article; however, it did not adopt the amendments of the leftist Group and the SAB, LMŠ, SD. In accordance with Rule 128 of the Rules of Procedure of the National Assembly, the Committee then voted on and adopted all the articles of the bill together.

In Levica, it is always important to note that when we receive a short-term bill, all alarms should be turned on and the matter should be thoroughly reviewed. However, if the law, which has already been in the parliamentary procedure with similar content, is complementary, then these alarms are even more justified. If most of the proposed amendments concern the regulation of the functioning of the Slovenian Representation in Eurojust and the arrangements of the duty stations and the preparation for the work of the state prosecutor's staff, a new proposal for greater supervision by the heads of the state prosecutor's office will be made. The first question is: Why is this change so urgent? Is the regulation really so flawed? The Government's unconvincing argument that a mandatory review of the prosecution's files on crimes that are at risk of at least three years' imprisonment will help to strengthen the uniformity of prosecution is all the more concerned. The reason for such a hurry is, of course, political. It is hidden in the coalition contract of the current government, in Chapter Public Administration and Justice, paragraph 12. This point provides for the amendment of the Public Prosecutor's Act in the direction of ventilation and co-signage of the Prosecutor's Office, and in the case of worse crimes, for groups of prosecutors, in the event of dismissal of criminal charges. The data provided by the Ministry of Justice show that different state prosecutors have a different number of dropped criminal charges. We can therefore welcome measures that will contribute to the unifying of practice. But we do not see the proposed solution as a measure to solve this problem, but as an instrument for greater control over the work of state prosecutors. The proposed amendment poses a risk of political pressure on state prosecutors. In view of recent developments and political pressures by the Government of the Republic of Slovenia on the Attorney-General's Office regarding the prosecution of protestors, such dependence on the decision-making of the leader can rightly concern us, as it can have a significant impact on the decisions of state prosecutors which should be of a professional nature. That is why it is all the more important to maintain the autonomy of the Attorney General. Otherwise, it may occur that the State prosecutor will have to initiate proceedings under pressure from the leader, even though he will see compelling reasons for rejecting the criminal charge. And given the number of criminal proceedings brought against visible members of the government coalition, we can be all the more legitimately concerned about how the proposed decisions will be implemented, the amendment of the law will be implemented in practice. In view of the pressures and our information mentioned above, that the main purpose of this law is to limit the autonomy of state prosecutors and to expose them as much as possible to the political pressure of the existing government, whose actors are also occasionally in criminal proceedings, all such systemic solutions must be strongly rejected. The amendment also maintains the possibility of pressure on individual state prosecutors to change their decision. Unfortunately, political pressure on the police and also on the state's prosecution is taking place under the current government. There are many examples of this. On 10 June 2020, such a secret meeting took place, witnessing how the rule of law is being built in Slovenia and how the rule of law, which has not been notified to the public or to the cabinet of President Janez Janša, is respected, is the visit of Attorney General Drag Šketa to the Prime Minister Ivan Janez Janša. The Prosecutor-General met with the Prime Minister of the Government immediately after the attack by Prime Minister Janez Janša on the prosecution and two weeks before the instructions of Minister Hojs to the Director-General of Police. The public has not been informed of this meeting. However, in the Supreme Public Prosecutor's Office, they say that they have no authority to interpret the law governing the organisation and work of the police. A week earlier, John Janez Janša asked Shketa to prosecute protestors for writing on banners and threatened him with direct responsibility if there were casualties. This way, the State Prosecutor's Council, under the leadership of Tamara Gregorčič, referred to as an unacceptable political pressure by the highest representative of the executive branch against the Attorney General. It is not known why they did not know about the meeting at the College of the Supreme State Prosecutors, whose members are individual Supreme and Senior Prosecutors; and the body which is key to the field of criminal law; and why the meeting was chaired by Ivan Janez Janša, President of the Government. In view of the pressure and our information that the main purpose of this law is to limit the autonomy of state prosecutors and to expose them as much as possible to the political pressure of the existing government, whose actors are also occasionally in criminal proceedings, all such systemic solutions must be strongly rejected; also, the amendment preserves the possibility of pressure on individual state prosecutors to change their decision. Thank you.
ParlaMint-SI_2020-09-29-SDZ8-Redna-20.ana.u7	Thank you very much. The word is given by the New Slovenia Group – Christian Democrats, in its name Blaž Pavlin.
ParlaMint-SI_2020-09-29-SDZ8-Redna-20.ana.u8	Thank you for your word. Slovenian justice has been facing the issue of increasing the proportion of rejected complaints for a long time. The average rate of rejected complaints increased by more than 56 % between 2005 and 2019. In 2005, it was still 39% and last year it was 61%. Such a high proportion of rejected complaints raises a legitimate question as to whether in our country the competent authorities are prosecuting crimes sufficiently aggressively and consistently. Such information makes it difficult to remain passive, and action is needed. The issue of the high proportion of rejected complaints in New Slovenia has been noted for some time. Among other things, we have addressed this issue in our last election program. With this law, we are now responding to this problem. Every prosecutor is bound by the principle of the legality of prosecution. This means that every prosecutor is obliged to initiate and prosecute any offence that is prosecuted under official responsibility. Therefore, any unjustified decision to dismiss the complaint at the same time constitutes a violation of one of the fundamental principles of criminal procedural law. The amendment's proposal brings in the law the incorporation of a stronger security guard, in order to prevent the country from unwarrantedly departing from the pursuit of worse crimes. The final decision to dismiss an accusation of worse crimes will not be taken by every prosecutor alone. The Head of Public Prosecutions will have to review any such decision and sign the draft discard act by signing it. I estimate that we will be able to contribute to a more unified and consistent prosecution of worse crimes. The fact that the Head of Public Prosecutions will have to be aware of the act of dismissal cannot constitute an intervention in the independence of the Prosecutor. Today, the law regulates the so-called institute of taking over cases in which the head of the District Attorney's Office can take over a case on his own. The novelty is only about strengthening cooperation and more intensive involvement of the Chief of Public Prosecutions in the decision to dismiss more important cases. I believe that the proposed solution will also contribute to a more unified approach to the handling of complaints. The rate of dismissed complaints varies considerably between the District Public Prosecutors' Offices and has ranged between 49% and 68% for several years. The bill also removes irregularities found by the Court of Auditors in the area of preparedness and on-call within the State Prosecutor's Office. The Public Prosecutor's Act is also aligned with the EU Regulation on the provisions governing the national representative in Eurojust. This is an agency dedicated to joint fight against the worst forms of organised crime in the European Union. In view of all of the above, the Members of New Slovenia will support the proposal of the law.

ParlaMint-SI_2020-09-29-SDZ8-Redna-20.ana.u10	Honourable President of the National Assembly, Honourable Minister, Secretary of State, Honourable Fellows and Fellow Members! The proposal for a Law on Amendments and Supplements to the Law on Public Prosecutions does not at first sight appear to be problematic. The applicable provisions of the law shall be adapted to comply with the content of the Regulation of the European Union on the European Union Agency for Judicial Cooperation in Criminal Matters. They shall also address irregularities identified by the Court of Auditors in the implementation and recording of preparedness and on-calls. In the eyes, however, there will be a third part of the changes relating to strengthening the Head of Public Prosecutions in ensuring the effectiveness and uniformity of prosecution. In view of the appearance of any controversial bill, the Government added one article which strongly interferes with the autonomy and autonomy of state prosecutors. Such practices, when the Government is discreetly trying to impose provisions that can be constitutionally disputed, we have been several times in recent months and we have warned them clearly and loudly. The government wanted to do this quietly, as they confirmed the reduced procedure for the consideration of the law. At the SAB stage, we have already found that paragraph 24 of the proposal is quite controversial, and we have pointed out this. This was also confirmed by the Legal and Legal Service. It also addressed the State Prosecutor's Council and the Supreme State Prosecutor's Office and expressed concern about the dispute under this Article. Article 24 of the proposal amending Article 169 of the Law on State Prosecutor's Office adds the Institute of Co-signatures of the Head of State Prosecutor's Office. All of this could not have been so strange and suspicious if the government of John Janša had not suggested it. We all know the position of the ruling party to the state of the Slovenian judiciary. They keep repeating how ineffective the state's attorney's office is, how many complaints they dismiss and so on. Already at the meeting of the Justice Committee, we have tabled an amendment to delete this article, which the coalition has expected to reject and, with its amendment, has softened the Government's first proposal. The representative of the Legal and Legislative Service also assessed at the adoption of the amendment that the issue of compliance with Article 135 of the Constitution remained. We do not agree to this in the Sab party, so today we will once again support the opposition's joint amendment to delete Article 24. We do not understand how, despite the opposition of the profession, the opinion of the Legislative and Legal Service and other coalition stakeholders can still insist on this change. This is an obvious intervention in the independence of state prosecutors, and such a change can also be contested from a constitutional point of view. Article 3 of the Law on State Prosecutors provides that state prosecutors are independent, are bound by the Constitution, the law and the general principles of international law and ratified international treaties in their work. The State Prosecutor's Office Act therefore lays down the substantive independence of the State Prosecutor's Office, which means that no one shall interfere with their decisions in specific matters. Under the current legislation, the Head of Public Prosecutions has the leverage to enable him to take care of a uniform policy of persecution. In particular, the issue of liability for lawful criminal proceedings will be raised at this point. Such a change confers on the Head of Public Prosecutions the responsibility for the final decision taken. Such a solution will also place a heavy burden on the Head of Public Prosecutions, which will have a negative impact on the duration of the forecasting procedures. In order to be able to assess the correctness of the decision of the State Prosecutor's Office, he will need to examine thoroughly all the material and documentation. In the SAB Group, we oppose such solutions. If the amendment to delete Article 24 at issue is not adopted, we will not support the law. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2020-09-29-SDZ8-Redna-20.ana.u12	Thank you for your word, Mr. President. A respected minister with a team, colleagues and colleagues! According to a proposal for a law that, while substantively pursuing three more important objectives, the State Prosecutor's Office will have some improvements in its work, and these can make a significant contribution to improving the efficiency of the State Prosecutor's Office. The first of the three substantive sets of amendments is the improvement of the labour law regime for the participation of state prosecutorial staff in the conduct of urgent procedural actions, thus implementing the Court of Auditors’ recommendations regarding the recording and payment of preparedness and on-call allowances. The proposal further implements European Union legislation on the functioning of the European Union Judicial Cooperation Agency in criminal proceedings. Thus, part of the bill is intended for a new procedure for the appointment of a national representative, his deputy and an assistant in Eurojust. Their mandate and rights, which go to designated representatives from the Republic of Slovenia, shall be redefined. The State Prosecutor's Office acts as an independent independent state authority whose main function is to detect crimes and prosecute perpetrators. Appropriate legislation is needed to enable public prosecutors to carry out their functions successfully. This is where we all agree. A key issue in today's proposal, however, is considered by some in this Chamber to revolve around the possible impact of leading prosecutors on the decisions of individual competent prosecutors in the cases they solve. The rule of law should also address the potential risk of policy impact on the work of the State Prosecutor's Office of the new proposed regulation of Article 169 of this proposal, which deals with the effectiveness and uniformity of prosecution. This is an assessment submitted by the proposed amendment to delete Article 24 in their explanation. In the Desus Group of the new Article 24, we do not perceive as a threat to the substantive independence of the State Prosecutor's Office, but as a step towards greater efficiency and a more uniform policy of persecution. According to our assessment, this is merely an upgrade to the existing obligations of the Prosecutor-General after the submission of the draft act to his direct supervisor, i.e. the Chief Prosecutor. In view of the statistics of the dismissed cases, as discussed in the introduction of the proposed legislative text, it is reasonable that the role of the Prosecutor-in-chief should be increased in those serious crimes where a higher prison sentence is imposed, and in addition to the Chief Prosecutor, two other prosecutors are included in the review of the decision to dismiss the case, which, in our opinion, means that both transparency and the independence of the assessment of the grounds for prosecution are ensured. The Ministry of Justice, in addition to high statistics on dismissed cases, also notes a derogation by number between the individual prosecutions and the proposed new model of so-called supervision of prosecutors is a necessary step towards a more uniform policy of prosecution. The Constitutional Court has also already called for the issue of control over the work of the State prosecutions, which states in one of its decisions that internal supervision of the work of the State prosecutor in order to ensure the uniformity of prosecution and the professional regularity and efficiency of the execution of the State prosecutor's office is not admissible and that the Prosecutor is obliged to follow the general instructions on the law enforcement policy formulated by the competent authority in accordance with the law. Therefore, in the Desus Group, we see no reason not to support the proposed law. As an additional argument of the apparent imposition of control or political influence on state prosecutors, another argument from the Constitutional Court is welcome, which concludes that the State Prosecutor is part of the executive branch of government, but not one that could be directed by the Government or any ministry in terms of political and professional guidance in specific matters. Therefore, in the Desus Group, we consider that the adoption of the law, in particular as regards Article 24 as contained in the amended proposal of the law, is not in any way detrimental to the independent and independent work of the state prosecutors. It is only a lever to make the work more efficient and the process of prosecution is well-managed and well-founded in one direction or another. Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-SI_2020-09-29-SDZ8-Redna-20.ana.u15	Thank you very much. The Member Group of the Slovenian Democratic Party has the floor. Franz Beznik.
ParlaMint-SI_2020-09-29-SDZ8-Redna-20.ana.u16	Dear Minister, respectable colleagues and colleagues! The Law on the State Prosecutor's Office has been amended several times since its adoption, but has not been systematically harmonised. Its individual institutes needed upgrades, which the profession noted in the joint reports on the work of state prosecutors and in the annual reports. The working group, composed of representatives of the Ministry of Justice, the State Attorney's Office of the Republic of Slovenia, the State Prosecutor's Council and the Association of State Prosecutors of Slovenia, has drawn up expert conclusions following the examination of the Institute of the State Prosecutor's Office and its partial novelties on the basis of the debate held on individual institutes, which have led to the following solutions. First; optimisation of the procedures for the appointment and promotion of state prosecutors, secondly; more specific regulation of the grounds for the termination of the state prosecutor's office, thirdly; strengthening the autonomy and responsibility of the heads of state prosecutors to carry out the tasks of the state prosecutor's office, fourthly; more comprehensive regulation of the status of the organisation and functioning of the State Prosecutor's Council, fifthly; upgrading of individual provisions or institutions, in particular the use of the national community language, suspension or suspension of the execution of the decision, disciplinary infringements, promotion of a higher title by law, appeal colleges, training for managerial functions, carrying out an examination of the provisions of the national law, judicial supervision, catalogue of competences of the Specialised State Prosecutor's Office and so on six occasions; facilitating the regulation of the content of the annual work programme and statistical reporting of the public prosecutions; The contents of Article 24 of the Government proposal, which amends Article 169 of the Law on the State Prosecutor's Office, have been disputed in the current proceedings of the law. The original government proposal, which is in law, lays down the obligation to submit a draft act in co-signed to the Head of State Prosecutors before the decision of the State Prosecutor to dismiss an action for a criminal offence for which a prison sentence is prescribed in the law for more than three years. By supplementing Article 169, the Prosecutor should submit a draft of this act to the Head of Public Prosecutions before a decision to dismiss an accusation, where the ground is that the alleged offence is not an offence for which the perpetrator is prosecuted under official responsibility, or if there is no reasonable suspicion that the suspect has committed an unknown offence. In the case of statutory penalties for more than eight years, the Head of Public Prosecutions shall consult two other state prosecutors on the grounds for rejecting this complaint. The proposed article retains the obligation to submit a draft act to the Chief of Public Prosecutions instead of the signature of the act of the proposal, now the contents, the following paragraph: "The Prosecutor may issue the act referred to in paragraph 1 and 2 of this Article only after the Head of State Prosecutor or the authorised person has signed the draft declaration that he has examined it." Why does the Slovenian Democratic Party and the proposer consider it important to amend Article 169 of the Law on the State Prosecutor's Office? Firstly, the draft rejected complaints were extremely high in relation to the analyses carried out. I think it's over 80% somewhere. Taking over the pursuit by a private person is very difficult, for him means quite high costs and, of course, personal engagement. The high number of discards raised the issue of efficiency and uniformity of prosecution, as not all courts in the same cases for the same reasons rejected criminal complaints on the same scale. There have been major differences in individual cases. Nor were the grounds for rejection always correct, which shows that timely and professional supervision of the work of individual prosecutors is essential when it is still possible to influence the decisions of the prosecution. This is, in fact, the only way to get things in order, even on the basis of the signature of the Head of Public Prosecutions, is to know that the draft was given for review or another opinion on the content, on the justification of rejection before the decision itself. It is therefore essential to amend the proposed content. The proposal for a law on amendments and additions to the Law on the State Prosecutor's Office, including the amendment included, will be supported by the Slovenian Democratic Party. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2020-09-29-SDZ8-Redna-20.ana.u18	Thank you, President, for your word. Good-bye, everybody! Today we have before us a new law that should be more or less implementing. Initially, the novel had two goals. The first objective was to prepare the implementing provisions of the Regulation on judicial cooperation in criminal matters. The second objective was to implement corrective measures resulting from the Court of Auditors’ audit report. So more or less technical amendments to the law. However, as we have seen many times before, we are also witnessing in the amendment of the State Prosecutor's Act a familiar mode of operation of this government, when laws that are not even contrary to the content do not need to be opposed are fuelled by controversial provisions that make the law unacceptable. For example, we have experienced this in every so-called corona package so far, and we are witnessing it today when this novel tries to significantly reduce the autonomy, autonomy and independence of state prosecutors in their work. You forget that the Public Prosecutor's Office is a constitutional category and that, even when it determines whether or not a criminal charge will be dropped or will continue to be prosecuted, it is only bound by the Constitution and the laws and internationally ratified treaties in its work. This is some international legal standard, some minimum, to make the country become and remain a legal state and to avoid any pressure on law enforcement authorities in this country. As soon as we allow the state prosecutors to decide to dismiss one of the criminal charges, so that in these cases the prosecutors will be subject to mandatory legal supervision and substantive interference in their professional decisions, we will allow politics to sneak into the work of prosecutors. And if you don't believe our warnings, listen to the profession for once for a change. The Committee on Justice has been very clear and unified with regard to the amendments on the supervision of prosecutors. The State Prosecutor's Council has, for example, taken the following position, quote: “We strongly oppose any regulation which, at some normative level, either directly or indirectly, affects the independence and independence of state prosecutors.” End of quote. Next, the State Attorney's Office said the following: “We appreciate that the proposal is also contrary to the principles of the rule of law and international standards on the functioning and position of state prosecutors in the exercise of the law enforcement function.” End of quote. I wonder, my fellow Members of the Coalition, which of these things is not understandable to you. In the LMŠ Group, we are truly concerned about where you are leading this country, by the time you whistle at the entire professional public, who in one voice shouts at you the same message. ParlaMint-SI_2020-09-29-SDZ8-Redna-20.ana.u20	Thank you for the floor, Mr President. Dear Madam Minister, colleague and colleague! It is clear that once again, as in recent times, under the cover of a reduced procedure in the proposal for a new law, there are provisions that are certainly not minor changes, but also, in this case, provisions that undermine the rule of law and are dangerously interfering with the independence and independence of the judiciary. It is true that the new Law on the State Prosecutor's Office, on the one hand, brings the necessary alignment with European Union law and also takes into account some of the recommendations made by the Court of Auditors, which we certainly agree with. However, the novel also brings about such changes, which cannot be supported by the Social Democrats in any way. Thus, if the State Prosecutor wishes to dismiss a criminal charge for a criminal offence for which a prison sentence has been prescribed for more than three years, he will have to obtain a co-signatory from the Head of the State Prosecutor before that; however, if he wishes to dismiss an indictment for a criminal offence for which a sentence of eight years or more has been imposed, the Chief Prosecutor will have to consult two other prosecutors. Of course, such a change has met with few criticisms. Although the coalition on the Justice Committee defended this solution and based it on a uniform policy of prosecution, it also tabled a coalition amendment that limited this signature to those criminal charges which were rejected for the reason that the Prosecutor considers that there are no signs of a crime that is being prosecuted under official duty and to those who do not have reasonable suspicion that the offence has been committed, the Legislative and Legal Service, the State Prosecutor's Council and the Supreme Prosecutor's Office have nevertheless strongly opposed such a proposal on the Committee. In our view, the amendment to this Act is questionable in terms of constitutionality, violates constitutional provisions on the independence and independence of state prosecutors who carry out their duties independently and must be protected from external pressure. And this co-signature is an unacceptable influence on the decisions of the state prosecutor in a specific case, as it will affect whether or not the prosecutor will withdraw from the prosecution. The Constitutional Court has already taken the view that the decision must be whether to bring a criminal charge before the competent court and how it will represent it, the matter of the state prosecutor who is handling the case. The representative of the Legislative and Legal Service pointed out on the Committee that the proposed new regime even deviates from the single law enforcement policy and that the effects of this provision cover both organisational and procedural issues. We also ask Social Democrats what this provision will bring into practice. The Chief of Public Prosecutions will have to examine the whole case or file before giving such a signature, which we know will, of course, delay the process of resolving the case. The proposed provision also violates international law, contrary to the Rome Charter and the positions of the Court of Justice of the European Union, according to which independence of judgment is an essential element of the independence of justice. Social Democrats point out that it is primarily the state that must ensure the independence and impartiality of the judiciary by setting clear and unambiguous rules that guarantee this independence. It must therefore do everything in its power to strengthen the institution of the rule of law, to strengthen people's confidence in the justice system and to establish appropriate and, in particular, legal mechanisms to control the judicial authorities and their decisions. The opposition parties were therefore proposing an amendment proposing the deletion of the disputed article in question. If the amendment in question does not receive support at today's meeting, then we will not support the law. Thank you.

At the time, however, the latter was not a novelty, but the marriage text merely specified the provisions of the 2004 former State Prosecutor's Order, which provided that the State Prosecutor could issue, file or execute the act only after the Head signed the draft diary stating that he had reviewed the draft. It will continue to be the case until now if the Head of State Prosecutor considers that the act of the competent state prosecutor contains shortcomings and incomprehensibility, it will not go forward, but will propose to the competent state prosecutor that it should be corrected. He will also propose a method of drafting an act. If the competent state prosecutor does not agree to supplement or correct the document and if the Head of State Prosecutor considers that he cannot agree with the draft act, the Head of State Prosecutor will take over the matter from the competent state prosecutor. The Advisory Council of European Prosecutors, in its 31st paragraph of this opinion, 2018, says: "Inside independence and autonomy does not mean that every prosecutor can do everything; it can be part of a hierarchical system whose task is clear and without prejudice to independence to ensure the proper functioning of the prosecution as a whole and the coherence of prosecution." And so on. Also in the following points, the Council clearly sets out the position that the leader has the right to give instructions in individual matters. All internal instructions within the framework of the Prosecutor's Office must be written, transparent and seek to find the truth and the proper functioning of justice. On the basis of the above arguments, we support the proposal of the SMC Group. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2020-09-29-SDZ8-Redna-20.ana.u30	Thank you very much. Your Honor! The discussion, both earlier by the leaders of the groups to amend the Prosecutor's Act and the current debate, shows that the key focus is on amending Article 169. The fact is that in the materials submitted, the Constitutional Court, in its judgment in 2013, pointed out that Article 135(1) of the Constitution of Slovenia contains the principle of functional independence of state prosecutors and that the amendment of this Article is clearly contrary. A similar assessment was made by the Legislative and Legal Service, as well as the Supreme Public Prosecutor's Office, to the current proposals for amendments to the Prosecutor's Act. These things have not yet been dismissed by the motioner when an introduction was introduced by the Minister of Justice, even now that some members of the government coalition have convinced us that the intentions are fair, positive, that certain results should be achieved. The thing is, obviously, as I said, it's an illicit pressure on prosecutors through a change in this law and an attack on the independence of the investigating authorities. And it's more than a professionally and politically motivated proposal. With the amendment of the coalition parliamentary groups, the applicants sought to mitigate in one more tangible way the original government proposal for a law, which was laid down, as previously stated, the obligation to submit a draft act for signature or co-signage to the Heads of State Prosecutor, prior to the decision of the State Prosecutor to dismiss an indictment for a criminal offence for which a prison sentence has been prescribed in the law for more than 3 years. By supplementing Article 169 of the Public Prosecutor's Act, that State prosecutor should submit a draft act to the Head of Public Prosecutions before the decision to dismiss the complaint, where the ground is for rejection. In the event of a statutory penalty of more than 8 years, the Head of Public Prosecutions, to whom this case has been submitted, shall then consult two state prosecutors on the grounds for rejection. The proposed article retains, now that it has been amended, i.e. in a newly prepared case, the obligation to submit a draft act to the Head of State Prosecutor's Office for inspection, only to sign the matter instead of a co-signature. This provides for the following paragraph: “The Prosecutor may issue the act referred to in paragraphs 1 and 2 of this Article only after the Head of the Public Prosecutor’s Office or the authorised person by signing the draft has indicated that he has examined it.” It is practically the same thing as it was prescribed in the previous amendment, which has changed. And such a proposed article is essentially no different from what I said before. There is still an element of the obligation here – to give the act to the Head of the State Prosecutor for review and, at the same time, to obtain his signature on the review of this draft act, which de facto amounts to the agreement of the Head of State Prosecutor. Without written evidence of the review of the act, the Prosecutor of State may not take a decision either to dismiss the complaint or to continue the criminal proceedings. The reasons are so largely the same as the reasons for the imposition of the co-signature of the State Prosecutor and the Head of Public Prosecutions. In this case, too, it is a matter of limiting the autonomy of state prosecutors. The proposal does not achieve the objectives – and this is essential – set out in the proposal, namely greater efficiency and uniformity of prosecution. Moreover, it also constitutes a significant restriction on the autonomy of the work of the State prosecutor, since in certain cases it requires the approval of the Head of State Prosecutor. This has been practically highlighted today by all parties, representatives of all parties who disagree with the proposed amendment. And the proposed regulation can directly and indirectly affect the independence and independence of state prosecutors, while at the same time it is questionable from a constitutional point of view and in contravention of international standards on the situation and functioning of state prosecutors in the exercise of law enforcement functions. The independence of state prosecutors is a guarantee for a fair, impartial and effective judiciary that protects the public and private interests of the persons involved. Public prosecutors must be autonomous in their decision-making and carry out their duties without external pressure, without being called upon to challenge you by the President of the Government, or interference, taking into account the principle of the division of power and responsibility; as announced by the President of the Government when he called on the Attorney General to prosecute protestors who carried the inscriptions with clear substantive significance. It follows from the provisions of the Public Prosecutor's Act that the state prosecutor's position is similar to that of a judge, which guarantees independence and independence to the state prosecutor. In spite of one organisational hierarchical arrangement, it is necessary to have the autonomy to decide individual criminal matters. The Slovenian state prosecutor is not an official, as we have pointed out several times before, but he is a judicial official with an unlimited mandate and a high level of responsibility for his work. The law itself conceptually provides for such a materially independent state prosecutor, which means that no one can and must not interfere with his decision, which is independent. But it is the Prosecutor of the State who is competent to determine, within the framework of the constitutional and legal framework, whether or not he will file a criminal charge and how he will represent it. This decision must be its own decision, which must be made conscientiously and according to its best professional judgement. This is not his right, but it is his responsibility, duty and responsibility in the execution of the state prosecutor's office. As I said at the outset, the Constitutional Court in 2013 pointed out in its judgment that Article 135(1) of the Constitution of Slovenia contains the principle of functional independence of state prosecutors; and this is precisely what we deny to amend this Article 169 of the Law. According to the Legislative and Legal Service of the National Assembly, the proposed article is controversial, as it means a departure from the Institute of the Single Law Enforcement Policy, which is under the jurisdiction of the Attorney General, and also contains framework guidelines for the rejection of criminal charges or for the use of either delayed prosecution or settlement. And the mandatory consultation of the Head of Public Prosecutions with the two state prosecutors on the grounds for dismissing the complaint also provides for more than 8 years of imprisonment, which includes a transfer of responsibilities and an assessment of the substantive decision. In view of the existing regime, it is no longer clear whether it is still an institution which is intended only to remedy the shortcomings or whether it is a procedure for non-conformity with the content of the decision. The Supreme State Prosecutor's Office also points out the inappropriateness of the proposed regime and, among other things, stresses that rejection does not constitute the end of the proceedings. In the event of rejection of the complaint, a subsidiary prosecution may also be possible, and rejection is not really judicata. The proposed arrangement does not address any possible discontent with the number of rejected complaints, but merely duplicates the work. This will also allow procedures to be severely delayed, may also lead to an ageing of persecution, or the required legitimacy simply cannot be carried out. And the proposed amendment poses a threat to one political pressure on state prosecutors, which has in particular taken place in contact between the Prime Minister of the Government and the Supreme Public Prosecutor with regard to the persecution of protestors who carry, as I have already said, certain passwords in their expression of dissatisfaction with the existing government. And given the latest developments and the political pressures of the government on the general prosecution of this prosecution, we can rightly be concerned that all of this will affect the decisions of the state prosecutors, which should be of a professional nature and not of a political nature dictated by the Cabinet – again, I say – of the President of the Government, which we have experience from the past. It is also even more important to maintain the autonomy of the State Prosecutor's Office. Otherwise, it will be the case that this state prosecutor, under pressure from the leader, will have to initiate a procedure that he himself would not initiate or drop a criminal complaint for compelling reasons. Nor is it clear from the explanation of the proposal how such centralised supervision of the work of state prosecutors could contribute to greater uniformity in the conduct of state prosecutors throughout the country. The single representation of state prosecutors shall be ensured by general instructions from the Attorney General, as provided for in Article 168 of the Public Prosecutor's Act, and by guidelines; and not by the supervision and handling of individual cases. Individual arbitral decision-making by the Head of Public Prosecutions may lead to even greater errors and, of course, this is also the case at hand. In the opinion of the Left, the proposed amendment does not in any way increase the effectiveness of prosecutions. If the Head of Public Prosecutions wishes to make the correct decision on the individual case he will receive in a mandatory review, he will have to examine the entire file in full, which requires time, which may mean an expiry of the case or a long-distance trial, which is contrary to the principles of effectiveness, even contrary to the principles of the right to a trial within a reasonable time. It is also clear from all of this that the proposed article reduces the level of the rule of law and creates the possibility of political pressure on state prosecutors. Therefore, the applicants of this amendment propose deleting Article 24. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2020-09-29-SDZ8-Redna-20.ana.u32	Thank you for your word. The aim of the proposed novel is to build on the effectiveness and unity of prosecution, and it is not a criticism or lack of confidence in the work of prosecutors, but the aim is to increase the confidence of the public and the victims in their decisions when they do not decide to prosecute. The independence of prosecutors must be understood in particular within the framework of Article 3 of the Public Prosecutor's Act. I also agree here with Ambassador Tina Heferle, who says that the illegal influence on the prosecutor is prohibited. However, let me point out that we are aware of this second paragraph of Article 3 and the entire ninth chapter of the Law on the State Prosecutor's Office. Although Article 3 of the State Prosecutor's Act explicitly provides for a prohibition of interference with the Prosecutor's specific decisions. However, if we read the rule to the end, we find that in the second paragraph there is an exception, these are general instructions and take over matters. The general instructions and the institute are therefore mechanisms which the State Prosecutor's System knows and defines as admissible interference with the specific decisions of the state prosecutors. Both mechanisms are explicitly referred to in Article 169 of the Public Prosecutor's Act. The opposition to the proposed amendment therefore appears to be a building block of the current regime. We must not overlook the fact that the proposed amendment is merely an upgrade of an already existing article, which, as such within the limits of admissibility and constitutional compliance by nature of things, represents an adjustment to the prescribed autonomy of state prosecutors. That internal control is permissible, the Constitutional Court has already stated in 2013. This shall be permissible for the purpose of ensuring uniformity of prosecution. We do not understand why the already existing possibility of issuing instructions and, therefore, the instructions issued, setting out a specific information system and thus providing for mandatory insight into any decision of the prosecutor, does not mean pressure and interference; now the proposed mandatory view merely to dismiss criminal charges to a limited extent represents this. Let us point out that the State Prosecutor's Council remains, as it has been, a defender of the prosecutor's independence. Article 3 of the Public Prosecutor's Act does not prejudice us, so it remains an open road for a state prosecutor who believes that he or she is in violation of his or her independence. Responsibility for effective and uniform prosecution is the primary responsibility of the leader. However, by nature, the leader is bound by the effectiveness of the performance of the function and therefore there is no need for the deadlines as previously laid down in which he will review the draft act of the individual prosecutor. In accordance with the principle of the legality of prosecution, the State Prosecutor is obliged to initiate and persist in prosecution if there is a reasonable suspicion that the person concerned has committed a criminal offence for which the perpetrator is prosecuted under an official obligation. However, when the State Prosecutor decides to reject, which is a derogation from the principle of legality, it is the responsibility of the Head of State Prosecutor to ensure the uniformity of prosecution; this is in accordance with the applicable regulation, including the assessment of the existence or absence of legal conditions for the initiation or continuation of prosecution. All of the above strengthens us in the conviction that the proposed amendment does not interfere with the independent decision-making of the state prosecutor, but rather that the levers of the leaders are aimed at ensuring the uniformity of the prosecution. The Attorney-General has already set out in his policy of prosecution that, with a view to unifying the prosecution's practice and properly directing the police, it is necessary to establish a continuous monitoring of negative decisions on prosecution, which have been taken on the grounds that there is no reasonable suspicion that a disproportionateity between the weight of the act and the consequences of the prosecution has been given and that it is not a crime. All of this is already the practice of guides or should be the practice of guides, as even the current policy of persecution has given particular attention, i.e. in terms of negative decisions. At this point, I would like to stress in particular that the criminal procedure is intended to eliminate the material and mental harm suffered by the victim because of the crime, for example in the form of restitution, compensation or satisfaction. However, it also has a retaliatory nature under which the State imposes a fair penalty on the perpetrator of the crime. In order for the State to fulfil its task, the State Prosecutor must also carry out his task, which is to prosecute the perpetrators of crimes. Where the State prosecutor does not decide to prosecute, the State cannot, by virtue of the judicial branch of power, judge whether someone has committed a crime or punish the perpetrator of a crime. In fact, the law provides for a means by which the situation of the victim is being corrected, which has already been exposed today. This is the fact that the victim can be prosecuted, so the victim has the right to be prosecuted. At the same time, we must be aware of the situation in which the victim is. It could have suffered such mental wounds that have not yet healed, so it is difficult to assume that it will take over the persecution if it considers it necessary to prosecute the perpetrator of the crime. On the other hand, it is assumed that the victim has sufficient time, energy, patience and money for the prosecution carried out by an authorized lawyer. We believe that we must not allow our rights to be best protected by those who have money or the most powerful, so we must allow all victims to maintain their dignity, which is also one of the fundamental human rights and freedoms. A further review of the reasons for the rejection of prosecution will give rise to greater weight and increase the victim's confidence in the decision of a state prosecutor. Let us stress that the proposed amendment does not stem from a lack of confidence in the work of the state prosecutor's office, but rather from its purpose, the aim of increasing confidence. Taking into account the above-mentioned and based on the fact that the proposed intervention in Article 169 of the Public Prosecutor's Act is merely its upgrade, which, as such, represents a correlation correction to the law of persecution, the latter being one of the key guides on which general instructions are also drawn up. In addition, the policy of prosecution is also present in the essential activities of the state prosecutor's office. We therefore believe that the proposal to amend Article 169 remains not only within the framework of permissible regulation but, as such, also necessary. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2020-09-29-SDZ8-Redna-20.ana.u36	Thank you for your word. Let me reiterate that the proposed amendment does not constitute a derogation from the established system. This system was already in force by Article 169 in 2011, so it has been in force for a number of years. The Constitutional Court also stated in its judgment in 2013 that, of course, there is no permissible command, prohibition or other instruction in specific cases. This does not mean, however, that internal control over the work of the State prosecutor would not be allowed in order to ensure the uniformity of prosecution and the professional regularity and efficiency of the performance of the state prosecution function. Nor does it mean that the State Prosecutor is not obliged to follow the general guidelines on law enforcement policies drawn up by the competent authority in accordance with the law. This arrangement has been in force since 2011. Let me say that the first paragraph of Article 169 did not, in fact, begin to be taken into account and implemented until 2016, when, at the initiative of the Ministry of Justice, which found that this article was not implemented, the heads of the District Attorneys' Offices adopted general instructions on those matters which they were obliged to submit in respect of the case before they concluded or issued the act. It is also very detailed in Article 90 of the Rules of Procedure as to how they should act in this case. The title already states that they are obliged to submit a draft act for review; and says that before a decision on the dropped complaint or in other cases when they take a decision, the signed draft of their act or the record of the order in the internal reminder, together with the file of the case, must be submitted to the supervisor for review. The Head shall indicate by signature on the draft and in the internal act that he has reviewed the draft or order and does not oppose it. Therefore, the registrar must not carry out the Prosecutor's order unless the Head of the case has indicated it in such a way. So the leader can now reject the signing of the State Prosecutor's Act or Order, the order in the internal reminder, because he considers that the draft or order is incomprehensible, flawed; and he makes an official note on this. At the same time, it sets out the reasons for his decision and gives guidance to the prosecutor as to how to remedy the shortcomings. According to the Code of State, it can be repeated once again, and the second time it is done, it can take over the matter and has to be assigned to someone else, or it can take over itself. And here is the legal protection afforded by the state prosecutor, who disagrees with the decision of the leader, and can submit, that is to say, a legal remedy to the State Prosecutor's Council. Once again, the provision proposed for adoption does not constitute a prohibition in specific cases, but constitutes an organisational remedy and an organisational measure on the basis of a provision which is general and abstract and covers an indefinite number of cases in which state prosecutors are also obliged, in accordance with a decision of the Constitutional Court, to comply with general rules. And, of course, their independence is limited when the matter is taken over and not followed in accordance with the instructions of the Head of Public Prosecutions. With regard to the likelihood that matters will therefore become obsolete and that the decision-making principle will be violated within a reasonable time frame, let me say that the single law of prosecution already sets out the duty to monitor completely all cases which are dismissed, irrespective of the level of the penalty at stake, for three reasons: where the state prosecutor finds that there is no evidence of a criminal offence, that there is no reasonable suspicion or that criminality is excluded. There have been 18,000 such cases in the past year. The proposal, however, proposes that such control be carried out or that an inspection be carried out of a criminal offence for which more than three years is at risk, for only two reasons, where there is no evidence of a crime or there is no reasonable suspicion. These cases represent around 40% of all cases dismissed, which means that in this case there would be 2 000 780 cases. Since the heads of the District Attorneys' Offices of the State in 2016, 13, have already adopted general instructions on the basis of which they review the cases, for example, the District Attorney's Office reviews all cases which are ad acta, or cases which are closed, all cases in which the Prosecutor or the Prosecutor makes a written statement of withdrawal from prosecution, all cases in which the Prosecutor, the Prosecutor, submits an indictment without an application for a criminal order. For example, the Second District Attorney's Office has 7 of these types of crimes, and the following are 9, possibly for the Special Prosecutor's Office. Therefore, one copy must be made available to the Chief of Public Prosecutions, before the decision is taken, so that one copy is made available to the Office of the Head, i.e. because of offences of abuse of position or trust in economic activity under Article 240 and other offences committed against banks in which the perpetrator performed a managerial or supervisory function; and it is prescribed to what extent. In short, on the basis of the general instructions, the whole lot is already listed by the District State Prosecutors, which cases must be brought for review. So it is a fully established practice, which is now in force; and in no way interferes with the independence of the state prosecutor's office, and the leader is also responsible and responsible for the expertise and regularity of the decision of the state prosecutor, and also for the timely resolution of cases. And second, more to the so-called serious crimes, the proposal to the investigating judge to give consent to the filing of a direct indictment, of course, by the prosecution. I must admit that it would be difficult to add anything to this, but I firmly believe that such a change is also necessary. As has been said in the Group of Members, I personally strongly support this change, and I believe that in the political debate, at least by some opposition Members, it is not right to ignore this important segment, because within the State Prosecutor's Office they are independent. We are aware that every prosecutor is bound by the principle of the legality of prosecution. This means that every prosecutor is obliged to initiate and prosecute any offence that is prosecuted under official responsibility. Therefore, any unjustified decision to dismiss the complaint at the same time constitutes a violation of one of the fundamental principles of criminal procedural law. The proposal for a novel that we are considering brings into law the incorporation of a stronger security guard, which we want to prevent the country from unwarrantedly moving away from the pursuit of worse crimes. Worse crimes. The final decision to dismiss an accusation of worse crimes will not be taken by every prosecutor on his own. The Head of Public Prosecutions will have to review any such decision and, of course, sign a draft discard act. I estimate that we will be able to contribute to a more unified and consistent prosecution of worse crimes. The fact that the Head of Public Prosecutions will have to be informed of the drop-off act cannot constitute an intervention in the independence of the Prosecutor. Even today, the law regulates the so-called institute of taking over cases in which the head of the District Attorney's Office can take up a case on his own. The novelty is only about strengthening cooperation and more intensive involvement of the Head of Public Prosecutions in the decision to dismiss important cases. I believe that the proposed solution will also contribute to a more unified approach to the handling of complaints. The rate of dismissed complaints varies considerably between the District Public Prosecutors' Offices and has been fluctuating between 49 and 68 % for years. Here too, colleagues and colleagues, we cannot talk just about some statistical error. Finally, the proposal for a law also brings an end to the irregularities identified by the Court of Auditors in the area of preparedness and on-call within the State Prosecutor's Office. This is also an important content of this novel of the Law on the State Prosecutor's Office. Members of the New Slovenia amendment of the opposition, which proposes that paragraph 24 be deleted, will not be supported. As we have already announced, we will support the marriage. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2020-09-29-SDZ8-Redna-20.ana.u42	Thank you for your word. Let me point out once again that the Constitutional Court has already said that internal control is permissible, so we believe that the proposed amendment does not affect the independence of state prosecutors. With regard to independence, the Constitutional Court has pointed out that the State Prosecutor must, in concrete proceedings, respect the principle of equality before the law referred to in Article 14, paragraph 2, of the Constitution, based on the policy of prosecution of perpetrators of crimes, as laid down within the organisation of state prosecutors. This does not mean, therefore, that internal control over the work of the State prosecutor would not be allowed in order to ensure the uniformity of prosecution and the professional regularity and efficiency of the execution of the state prosecution function. Nor does it mean that the Attorney General is not obliged to follow the general guidelines on law enforcement policy. The purpose of the proposed novel is to maintain the autonomy of state prosecutors, which is already provided for in Article 3 of the State Prosecutor's Act. There is also a second paragraph concerning the exercise of the independence of state prosecutors, which also provides for exceptions. This means that the current regime provides for the views of the Heads of State Prosecutors in the general instructions in accordance with Article 169 of the Law on the State Prosecutor's Office, in accordance with this second paragraph. Our proposal is without prejudice to any decision. Today we only talk about mandatory insights and nothing else. So it's just an organizational norm. Everything else remains, as is already the case in the current law, and does not represent any deviation from the purpose of the law adopted in 2011. What we have now determined is that we have raised the rule of the State Prosecutor's Order to the legal level. It is said that this view is marked because, for example, it is not foreseen at the legal level, and this is the only one that represents a derogation from the current regime. I would point out, however, that these views are already taking place on the basis of the general guidelines adopted by the Head of State Prosecutors. These insights according to our data are based on instructions adopted by all state prosecutors. The current proposal relates only to the national situation, that is discarding. It is said that the circumstances arising from the procedural decision of each state prosecutor. The current guidelines, however, provide for insights essentially into any decision taken by each state prosecutor. Once again, I would like to make it clear that this arrangement does not affect our independence but only provides for additional leverage for the heads of state prosecutors. We are aware that the trend of rejection of complaints is increasing. The current levers available to prosecutors are not sufficient or strong enough. It is an upgrade to the current regime, an organisational measure which is primarily intended to take into account guidelines for the uniform and effective implementation of criminal prosecution. This also achieves the same level of legal predictability and thus the legal security of every individual in the country. The decision, as it has been so far, on a specific case shall be taken by the Prosecutor of State and shall be made by the Registrar with a view to ensuring uniformity and effectiveness of the prosecution. Let me point out that, already under the current regime, state prosecutors must inform the leader of the situation and the resolution of particularly important and challenging cases, indicating the measures taken and planned. All of this is practically already the practice of guides or should be the practice of guides, which must take into account the policy of prosecution when establishing the criteria for the effectiveness of prosecutions by state prosecutors. The Head of State Prosecutor's Office is responsible for the professional correctness and timeliness of the conduct of the State Prosecutor's Office, and should also ensure that the expected time of resolution of the matter is not exceeded. Such dynamics and the organisation of the work process can also be ensured through other organisational measures, so as not to give rise to delays for this reason. We are talking about a smaller proportion of cases, but at the same time it is a worse crime where the decision of the Attorney General must be fully weighed. Let me also mention that, according to the Committee of Ministers, the Council of Europe has also recognized, as an acceptable lever for the effectiveness of prosecution, the external, immediate supervision of the judiciary over the decisions of prosecutors. Therefore, our proposed legal solution to strengthen the effectiveness and unity of prosecution has remained within the legal and constitutional framework and within the framework of the State Prosecutor's Office, as it strengthens the leadership's position. Of course, the challenges for the state prosecutor's office remain, and everything will not be solved by laws and by-laws. Here comes the commitment to the profession of the State Prosecutor's Office, the management of the mission to work for the benefit of our society and to protect our rights. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2020-09-29-SDZ8-Redna-20.ana.u46	Dear Vice-Presidents and Members of Parliament, you are most welcome! The amendment to the Defence Act, which is before you today, regulates the status of soldiers after 45 years. This law also guarantees the social security of soldiers after 45 years. How do we provide for social security under this law on defence? In two ways. One thing is that those who occupy deficit formations, or special specialists who are needed, can continue to do their work even when they are 45 years old. For all others, it is envisaged that they will be able to re-engage with other government authorities in order to transfer wage classes with them. As an interesting thing to say, when I was getting information on how, say, pay grade cuts to soldiers who were too busy in other state bodies. For example, a soldier who was overworked in 2011 was in the 31st grade as a soldier, then in the 21st grade, so due to his overemployment he fell by 10 pay grade. For example, a soldier who was too busy in 2012 fell from the 24th to the 14th grade. I can go to a slightly newer period and check 2015, where the soldier had a 28th salary class; when he got hired, he fell to 21, that's for 7 pay grade. This is what we want to prevent soldiers from losing part of their salary when employed by other government authorities in order to lose their paid grade. This seems fair to us, and in some ways it is imperative that this is to the very least extent a sign of gratitude, that the country recognises to them, for their service to their homeland, that they can also transfer wage classes to their new job. The debate also raised the question why we should not extend the possibilities of operation and service to all after 45 years. The answer is that because we want young soldiers, we want a young army; and that is why this is the only way to set matters straight in order to extend our activities in the Slovenian Army to those that the military urgently needs; and for all others, we provide appropriate and appropriate services; that is to say, in a way that they do not lose their personal income. There has also been an extensive debate on an article that discusses the possibility for contractual reserves to remain members of political parties. Here I must point out that there is no news, even so far there has been no verification of membership in political parties, which in some sense stems from history, even from the time when military service was compulsory, usually those who served military service have also become reserves; and it has never been checked who was a member of a political party in the reserve. However, the Constitution clearly stipulates that when it comes to professional composition, professional soldiers are not allowed to join political parties. And here too is all fear, because also the Service Act of the Slovenian Army in the special article 16 prohibits any political action within the Slovenian Army. In this way, by adopting today's law, we will all together guarantee social security, better predictability, so that I will be extremely grateful to all those Members and Members who will support this law today.

Matthew Tonin, who presented the main objectives and main solutions to the bill. The basic objective of the bill is to introduce changes in the completion of the permanent composition and voluntary contractual reserve of the Slovenian Armed Forces, which will ensure the interest of candidates for employment in the Slovenian Army and for the contractual provision of military service in the Slovenian Armed Forces reserve, ensure programmed retention of personnel and stop the reduction of the Slovenian Armed Forces staff. Among the key solutions to the bill, the Minister highlighted the preservation of the rights of soldiers after 45 years of age, who, in accordance with the legislation so far, ceased their military duty. The panellists of the coalition groups of the Slovenian Democratic Party, the parties of the modern centre, New Slovenia, the Democratic Party of the Pensioners of Slovenia and the opposition Socialist Groups and the Alenka Bratušek Party expressed their support for the legal solutions; while representatives of the other opposition groups of the List of Marjan Sarca and the Left expressed their support for the bill or strongly opposed the bill. The amended bill is an integral part of this report. Thank you very much.

The trend in the reduction of defence expenditure was halted when the Government of the Modern Centre Party adopted a decision on a continuous increase in defence resources in the next medium term. Secondly, for members of the contract reserve, the novel introduces salaries equivalent to their current salary in the current service. Thirdly, the novel also renews the framework for basic military training, which may take less than three months after the new one. The human resources issue in the Slovenian Army is indeed very difficult, as is evident from the following data. The number of members of the permanent composition has declined since 2012, on average for more than 100 members per year. In 2011, 7 000 567 permanent members were employed in the Slovenian Army, and in July this year only 6 000 321. Similar trends exist in contractual reserves.
